We need 4 `matrix_b` fragments of shape 32x16 (for the 4 N groups). - Accumulators: 2x4 = 8 fragments of shape 16x16. - For each sub-step, we load the appropriate `matrix_a` and `matrix_b` fragments from shared memory and call `wmma::mma_sync`. This is a standard WMMA GEMM kernel. **Shared memory layout:** - `sA` size `BM * BK` = `128 * 64` = 8192 bytes. - `sB` size `BK * BN` = `64 * 256` = 16384 bytes. - Total = 24576 bytes (24KB). - We can add `__shared__ int8_t sA[BM*BK]; int8_t sB[BK*BN];`. - Since we need a double buffer for global->shared loads to overlap with compute, we can allocate double the size: 48KB. That's well under 228KB. **Global memory loading:** - Each block loads a `BM x BK` tile of A and a `BK x BN` tile of B. - Coalesced loads: For A (row-major), threads load consecutive elements in a row. With 512 threads, each thread loads (BM*BK)/512 = (8192)/512 = 16 int8 elements. That's 4 `int4` loads per thread (each `int4` is 4 bytes = 4 int8). Good. - For B (column-major), threads load consecutive elements in a column? Actually column-major storage means columns are contiguous. To load a tile of `BK x BN`, a thread should load consecutive elements along the K dimension (rows). Since columns are contiguous? No, in column-major, element (k,n) is at offset `k + n*K`. So for fixed n, elements along k are contiguous. So loading a column segment of length BK is contiguous. If we have 512 threads, we can map them to load the tile in a coalesced manner: thread idx maps to linear offset in the BK x BN tile. Since each column of length BK is contiguous, linear indexing is fine. The leading dimension for B in global memory is K (4096). So when loading a tile, after reading BK elements, the next column is at stride K. As long as threads in a warp read contiguous addresses within a column, it's coalesced. Mapping thread id to (k, n) such that adjacent threads read adjacent k within the same n is ideal. For example, thread linear id `tid` loads element at offset `tid` within the tile, but in column-major layout, the offset is `k + n*K`. If we map `tid` to `k = tid % BK`, `n = tid / BK`, then threads in a warp will have varying `n`. If BK=64, a warp of 32 threads will have `n` values 0..0 for first 64 threads, but we only have 32 threads, so all will be in n=0 and read contiguous k. That's coalesced. For the next warp, n increases. So it's fine. - We can use `char4` to load 4 int8 per thread to maximize bandwidth. **Double buffering / Pipelining:** - H100 benefits from async copy. We can use `cp.async.cg.shared.global` to copy global to shared asynchronously. But `cp.async` requires compute capability >= 8.0. Hopper supports it and also supports `cp.async.bulk`. However, using `cp.async` via inline PTX or `cuda::memcpy_async` (CUDART) is easier. CUDA 11.1+ provides `cuda::barrier` and `memcpy_async` in `<cuda_pipeline.h>` or `<cuda/barrier>`. For simplicity, we can do synchronous loads with vectorized `char4` and then `__syncthreads()`. The reference is a 2-stage CUTLASS kernel with pipeline. But I'm not sure if `load_inline` includes that header by default? We can include it. However, pipelining in software (double buffering) is straightforward with shared memory: load tile k into smem buffer 0, while computing on buffer 1. We need two buffers for A and B. The challenge is overlapping the global memory load latency with compute. For compute-bound GEMM, this is important. But with large enough warps and shared memory bandwidth, maybe software pipelining with `__syncthreads()` is enough. Let's just write the int8 kernel with int32 accumulation. **Kernel code outline:** ```cuda #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <mma.h> using namespace nvcuda; #define BM 128 #define BN 256 #define BK 64 #define WM 32 #define WN 64 #define MMA_M 16 #define MMA_N 16 #define MMA_K 32 __global__ void gemm_kernel( const int8_t *A, const int8_t *B, const int32_t *C, int32_t *D, int M, int N, int K, int32_t alpha, int32_t beta) { __shared__ int8_t sA[BM * BK]; __shared__ int8_t sB[BK * BN]; int bm = blockIdx.x * BM; int bn = blockIdx.y * BN; int warpId = threadIdx.x / warpSize; int laneId = threadIdx.x % warpSize; // Warps per block: 4x4 const int warpsM = 4; const int warpsN = 4; int warpRow = warpId / warpsN; int warpCol = warpId % warpsN; // Declare fragments wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, MMA_M, MMA_N, MMA_K, int8_t, wmma::row_major> aFrag[WM / MMA_M]; // 2 wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, MMA_M, MMA_N, MMA_K, int8_t, wmma::col_major> bFrag[WN / MMA_N]; // 4 wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, MMA_M, MMA_N, MMA_K, int32_t> cFrag[WM / MMA_M][WN / MMA_N]; // Initialize accumulators #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) wmma::fill_fragment(cFrag[i][j], 0); // Outer K loop for (int bk = 0; bk < K; bk += BK) { // Load A tile to shared memory // Each thread loads 16 int8 (one int4) int a_tid = threadIdx.x; int a_total = BM * BK; int a_elems_per_thread = a_total / blockDim.x; // 16 int a_start = a_tid * a_elems_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < a_elems_per_thread / 16; ++i) { int idx = a_start + i * 16; int m = idx / BK; int k = idx % BK; int4 val = *reinterpret_cast<const int4*>(&A[(bm + m) * K + (bk + k)]); *reinterpret_cast<int4*>(&sA[idx]) = val; } // Load B tile to shared memory // B is column-major in global: B[k + n*K] // Store in shared as sB[k + n*BK] (col-major) int b_total = BK * BN; int b_elems_per_thread = b_total / blockDim.x; // 32 int b_start = a_tid * b_elems_per_thread; // reuse a_tid #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < b_elems_per_thread / 16; ++i) { int idx = b_start + i * 16; int k = idx % BK; int n = idx / BK; int4 val = *reinterpret_cast<const int4*>(&B[(bk + k) + (bn + n) * K]); *reinterpret_cast<int4*>(&sB[idx]) = val; } __syncthreads(); // Inner loop over K tile #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < BK; k += MMA_K) { // Load A fragments #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) { int m = warpRow * WM + i * MMA_M; const int8_t *aPtr = &sA[(m) * BK + k]; wmma::load_matrix_sync(aFrag[i], aPtr, BK, wmma::mem_row_major); } // Load B fragments #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) { int n = warpCol * WN + j * MMA_N; const int8_t *bPtr = &sB[k + n * BK]; wmma::load_matrix_sync(bFrag[j], bPtr, BK, wmma::mem_col_major); } // MMA #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) { #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) { wmma::mma_sync(cFrag[i][j], aFrag[i], bFrag[j], cFrag[i][j]); } } } __syncthreads(); } // Apply alpha #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) { #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) { #pragma unroll for (int t = 0; t < cFrag[i][j].num_elements; ++t) { cFrag[i][j].x[t] *= alpha; } } } // Store to D // D is row-major MxN #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) { #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) { int m = bm + warpRow * WM + i * MMA_M; int n = bn + warpCol * WN + j * MMA_N; int32_t *dPtr = &D[m * N + n]; wmma::store_matrix_sync(dPtr, cFrag[i][j], N, wmma::mem_row_major); } } // If beta != 0, apply epilogue with C // But we can skip if beta==0. To be safe: if (beta != 0) { // Each thread handles some elements of the block tile // Not needed for this problem but we can include a simple loop int total = BM * BN; int elems = total / blockDim.x; int start = threadIdx.x * elems; for (int idx = start; idx < start + elems; ++idx) { int m = idx / BN; int n = idx % BN; int gi = (bm + m) * N + (bn + n); D[gi] += beta * C[gi]; } } } ``` Wait, there is an issue with the beta epilogue: `wmma::store_matrix_sync` writes from a warp. Therefore, we'll create B and C with this helper. But we need to handle the layout. The source creates B as `problem_size.kn()` which is (K, N) with `LayoutInputB = ColumnMajor`. In CUTLASS HostTensor, column-major means the tensor view has shape (K, N) but strides (1, K). So the linear index of element (k,n) is `k + n*K`. When we fill with `TensorFillRandomUniform`, it enumerates elements in the order of the view's capacity, which is probably linear memory order: row-major for the underlying storage? In `TensorFillRandomUniform`, it likely does: ```cpp for (size_t i = 0; i < view.capacity(); ++i) { view.at(i) = random_func(rng); } ``` `view.at(i)` accesses the i-th element in the storage order (colexicographic or lexicographic depending on layout?). Actually CUTLASS `TensorView` has `at(Index coord)` and `at(size_t idx)`. The linear `idx` is the offset in the underlying storage. For column-major, storage order is column by column. So `idx` maps to memory offsets sequentially. So the random numbers are written in storage order: for n=0..N-1, for k=0..K-1, B[k + n*K] = rand(). So the linear sequence of random numbers corresponds to column-major layout. Thus, if we allocate a PyTorch tensor of shape (K, N) in column-major format (or shape (N, K) row-major) and fill its flat data pointer sequentially with random numbers using `std::mt19937`, we will match the memory layout. In PyTorch, we can create a tensor `t = torch.empty(K, N, dtype=torch.int8)` which is row-major by default. But we can create `t = torch.empty(N, K, dtype=torch.int8)` and fill it flat. Then treat it as column-major (K,N) by indexing `k + n*K`. That is what we planned. Alternatively, we can create a tensor of shape (K, N) with `torch.empty_strided((K, N), (1, K))` to get column-major strides! Yes! `torch.empty_strided((K, N), (1, K), dtype=torch.int8)` will allocate a column-major tensor in PyTorch! Then we can fill its `.data_ptr()` sequentially and it matches exactly the (K,N) column-major memory layout. This is much cleaner. Similarly, C is (M, N) row-major. `torch.empty(M, N, dtype=torch.int32)` is already row-major. We can fill it flat. A is given as row-major (M, K). So we can pass it directly. Thus: - B = `torch.empty_strided((K, N), (1, K), dtype=torch.int8).cuda()`. - C = `torch.empty(M, N, dtype=torch.int32).cuda()`. - Fill them using our C++ helper that writes to the data pointer sequentially with `std::mt19937(1)` and `std::uniform_int_distribution<int>(-4,4)`. We need to ensure that the C++ helper is called from Python. We can expose it via pybind11 in the inline extension. Now, let's write the Python code. ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline cpp_source = r''' #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <mma.h> #include <random> using namespace nvcuda; #define BM 128 #define BN 256 #define BK 64 #define WM 32 #define WN 64 #define MMA_M 16 #define MMA_N 16 #define MMA_K 32 #define WARPS_M 4 #define WARPS_N 4 __global__ void gemm_int8_wmma( const int8_t * __restrict__ A, const int8_t * __restrict__ B, const int32_t * __restrict__ C, int32_t * __restrict__ D, int M, int N, int K, int32_t alpha, int32_t beta) { __shared__ int8_t sA[BM * BK]; __shared__ int8_t sB[BK * BN]; int bm = blockIdx.x * BM; int bn = blockIdx.y * BN; int warpId = threadIdx.x >> 5; int warpRow = warpId / WARPS_N; int warpCol = warpId % WARPS_N; wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, MMA_M, MMA_N, MMA_K, int8_t, wmma::row_major> aFrag[WM / MMA_M]; wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, MMA_M, MMA_N, MMA_K, int8_t, wmma::col_major> bFrag[WN / MMA_N]; wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, MMA_M, MMA_N, MMA_K, int32_t> cFrag[WM / MMA_M][WN / MMA_N]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) wmma::fill_fragment(cFrag[i][j], 0); for (int bk = 0; bk < K; bk += BK) { // Load A tile int a_elems = BM * BK / blockDim.x; // 16 int a_offset = threadIdx.x * a_elems; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < a_elems / 16; ++i) { int idx = a_offset + i * 16; int m = idx / BK; int k = idx % BK; const int4 *src = reinterpret_cast<const int4 *>(&A[(bm + m) * K + (bk + k)]); int4 *dst = reinterpret_cast<int4 *>(&sA[idx]); *dst = *src; } // Load B tile int b_elems = BK * BN / blockDim.x; // 32 int b_offset = threadIdx.x * b_elems; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < b_elems / 16; ++i) { int idx = b_offset + i * 16; int k = idx % BK; int n = idx / BK; const int4 *src = reinterpret_cast<const int4 *>(&B[(bk + k) + (bn + n) * K]); int4 *dst = reinterpret_cast<int4 *>(&sB[idx]); *dst = *src; } __syncthreads(); #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < BK; k += MMA_K) { #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) { int m = warpRow * WM + i * MMA_M; wmma::load_matrix_sync(aFrag[i], &sA[m * BK + k], BK, wmma::mem_row_major); } #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) { int n = warpCol * WN + j * MMA_N; wmma::load_matrix_sync(bFrag[j], &sB[k + n * BK], BK, wmma::mem_col_major); } #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) { #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) { wmma::mma_sync(cFrag[i][j], aFrag[i], bFrag[j], cFrag[i][j]); } } } __syncthreads(); } // Apply alpha #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) { #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) { #pragma unroll for (int t = 0; t < cFrag[i][j].num_elements; ++t) { cFrag[i][j].x[t] *= alpha; } } } // Store accumulators to D #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) { #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) { int m = bm + warpRow * WM + i * MMA_M; int n = bn + warpCol * WN + j * MMA_N; wmma::store_matrix_sync(&D[m * N + n], cFrag[i][j], N, wmma::mem_row_major); } } // Apply beta if needed if (beta != 0) { __syncthreads(); int total = BM * BN; int elems = total / blockDim.x; int start = threadIdx.x * elems; #pragma unroll for (int idx = start; idx < start + elems; ++idx) { int m = idx / BN; int n = idx % BN; int gi = (bm + m) * N + (bn + n); D[gi] += beta * C[gi]; } } } torch::Tensor gemm_forward( torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, int32_t alpha, int32_t beta) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = B.size(1); auto D = torch::empty({M, N}, torch::dtype(torch::kInt32).device(A.device())); dim3 grid(M / BM, N / BN); dim3 block(WARPS_M * WARPS_N * 32); gemm_int8_wmma<<<grid, block>>>( A.data_ptr<int8_t>(), B.data_ptr<int8_t>(), C.data_ptr<int32_t>(), D.data_ptr<int32_t>(), M, N, K, alpha, beta); return D; } void fill_random_tensor(torch::Tensor t, int64_t seed, int min_val, int max_val) { int8_t* ptr = t.data_ptr<int8_t>(); int64_t numel = t.numel(); std::mt19937 gen(static_cast<uint32_t>(seed)); std::uniform_int_distribution<int> dis(min_val, max_val); for (int64_t i = 0; i < numel; ++i) { ptr[i] = static_cast<int8_t>(dis(gen)); } } PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) { m.def("gemm_forward", &gemm_forward, "GEMM int8 forward"); m.def("fill_random_tensor", &fill_random_tensor, "Fill tensor with random int8"); } ''' gemm_cuda = load_inline( name="gemm_cuda", cpp_sources=cpp_source, extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "--use_fast_math", "-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90"], functions=["gemm_forward", "fill_random_tensor"], ) M = 5120 N = 4096 K = 4096 ALPHA = 1 BETA = 0 class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, K, N, alpha, beta): super().__init__() self.alpha = alpha self.beta = beta # Create B as K x N column-major (strides (1, K)) self.register_buffer('B', torch.empty_strided((K, N), (1, K), dtype=torch.int8, device='cuda')) gemm_cuda.fill_random_tensor(self.B, 1, -4, 4) # C is M x N row-major, but beta=0 so values don't matter; fill with zeros self.register_buffer('C', torch.zeros(M, N, dtype=torch.int32, device='cuda')) if beta != 0: gemm_cuda.fill_random_tensor(self.C, 1, -4, 4) # Actually C is int32, fill_random expects int8. Problem. def forward(self, A): return gemm_cuda.gemm_forward(A, self.B, self.C, self.alpha, self.beta) ``` Wait, there are issues: 1. `fill_random_tensor` writes `int8_t` to the tensor's data pointer. std::uniform_int_distribution<int> dis(min_val, max_val); for (int64_t i = 0; i < numel; ++i) { ptr[i] = static_cast<int8_t>(dis(gen)); } } """ gemm_cuda = load_inline( name="hopper_gemm", cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE, extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "-gencode", "arch=compute_80,code=sm_80", "-gencode", "arch=compute_90,code=sm_90"], functions=["gemm_forward", "fill_random_tensor"], ) M = 5120 N = 4096 K = 4096 ALPHA = 1 BETA = 0 class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, K, N, alpha, beta): super().__init__() self.alpha = alpha self.beta = beta B_cpu = torch.empty_strided((K, N), (1, K), dtype=torch.int8) gemm_cuda.fill_random_tensor(B_cpu, 1, -4, 4) self.register_buffer('B', B_cpu.cuda()) self.register_buffer('C', torch.zeros(M, N, dtype=torch.int32, device='cuda')) def forward(self, A): return gemm_cuda.gemm_forward(A, self.B, self.C, self.alpha, self.beta) ``` Potential issue: `gemm_cuda` is defined at module load time. If compilation fails, the module won't load. But the evaluation will import the code and run it. So it must compile. Let's test compilation with `compile_kernel`. I'll create the exact string and pass it to the function. Wait, the `compile_kernel` function expects `kernel_code` as the full Python source. It probably writes it to a file and imports it. Our code contains raw CUDA strings and calls `load_inline`, which triggers compilation. If there's a compilation error, `compile_kernel` should return it. Let's call `compile_kernel` now. But before that, I want to check if `torch.empty_strided` works in all PyTorch versions. Yes, it's a standard function. Also, `torc ...` Actually, in the `fill_random_tensor` function, if `t` is a CPU tensor, `.data_ptr<int8_t>()` is fine. But if it's a GPU tensor, calling `.data_ptr<int8_t>()` and writing from host would segfault. We create `B_cpu` on CPU, so it's okay. Wait, `torch.empty_strided` creates a tensor with the specified strides. The storage size is computed as the maximum offset needed. For shape (K,N) and strides (1,K), the offset of element (K-1, N-1) is (K-1)*1 + (N-1)*K = K-1 + NK - K = NK - 1. So storage size is K*N. It's contiguous in column-major order. Now, let's call compile_kernel. I'll assemble the code string. One more thought: The `#if __CUDA_ARCH__ >= 800` guard inside the kernel means that for architectures below sm_80, the kernel body is empty. However, PyTorch might compile for sm_70. Will an empty kernel compile? Yes, but if launched, it does nothing. That's fine because we only run on sm_90. But nvcc may still compile the kernel for sm_70. Since the body is empty, no wmma support needed. But there is a subtle issue: The `wmma::fragment` types are used outside the `#if` guard? No, they are declared inside the `#if __CUDA_ARCH__ >= 800` block. The compiler parses the block for sm_90 but not for sm_70. So it's fine. However, `mma.h` might have some `static_assert` that triggers at include time, not instantiation time. I don't think so. It's usually safe. Let's compile.

Let me write the kernel: ```cpp #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <mma.h> using namespace nvcuda; #define BM 128 #define BN 256 #define BK 64 #define WM 64 #define WN 64 #define MMA_M 16 #define MMA_N 16 #define MMA_K 16 #define WARPS_M 2 #define WARPS_N 4 __global__ void __launch_bounds__(256) gemm_int8_wmma( const int8_t *__restrict__ A, const int8_t *__restrict__ B, const int32_t *__restrict__ C, int32_t *__restrict__ D, int M, int N, int K, int32_t alpha, int32_t beta) { // Shared memory __shared__ __align__(16) int8_t sA[BM * BK]; __shared__ __align__(16) int8_t sB[BK * BN]; int bm = blockIdx.x * BM; int bn = blockIdx.y * BN; int tid = threadIdx.x; int warpId = tid >> 5; int lane = tid & 31; int rowGroup = lane >> 2; // 0..7 int int4Id = lane & 3; // 0..3 // WMMA fragments wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, MMA_M, MMA_N, MMA_K, signed char, wmma::row_major> aFrag[WM / MMA_M]; wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, MMA_M, MMA_N, MMA_K, signed char, wmma::col_major> bFrag[WN / MMA_N]; wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, MMA_M, MMA_N, MMA_K, int> cFrag[WM / MMA_M][WN / MMA_N]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) wmma::fill_fragment(cFrag[i][j], 0); for (int bk = 0; bk < K; bk += BK) { // Load A to shared mem #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 2; ++i) { int rowBase = i * 64 + warpId * 8 + rowGroup; int globalRow = bm + rowBase; int globalCol = bk + int4Id * 16; int sA_offset = rowBase * BK + int4Id * 16; const int4 *src = reinterpret_cast<const int4*>(A + globalRow * K + globalCol); int4 *dst = reinterpret_cast<int4*>(sA + sA_offset); *dst = *src; } // Load B to shared mem #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < (BN / (WARPS_M * WARPS_N * 32 / 4)); ++i) { // 256/(32/4)? Let me recalculate. ``` Wait, let's calculate iterations for B. We have 8 warps. Each warp loads 8 columns per iteration (since 32 threads/4 = 8 columns). BN = 256 columns. So 256 / 8 = 32 columns per warp total? No. Wait, each warp handles 8 cols per iteration of the inner load. How many iterations? Total columns = 256. So 4 iterations. ```cpp #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 4; ++i) { int colBase = i * 64 + warpId * 8 + rowGroup; int globalCol = bn + colBase; int globalRow = bk + int4Id * 16; int sB_offset = colBase * BK + int4Id * 16; const int4 *src = reinterpret_cast<const int4*>(B + globalRow + globalCol * K); int4 *dst = reinterpret_cast<int4*>(sB + sB_offset); *dst = *src; } __syncthreads(); // Compute int warpRow = warpId / WARPS_N; int warpCol = warpId % WARPS_N; #pragma unroll for (int k = 0; k < BK; k += MMA_K) { #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) { int m = warpRow * WM + i * MMA_M; wmma::load_matrix_sync(aFrag[i], reinterpret_cast<signed char*>(sA + m * BK + k), BK, wmma::mem_row_major); } #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) { int n = warpCol * WN + j * MMA_N; wmma::load_matrix_sync(bFrag[j], reinterpret_cast<signed char*>(sB + k + n * BK), BK, wmma::mem_col_major); } #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) { #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) { wmma::mma_sync(cFrag[i][j], aFrag[i], bFrag[j], cFrag[i][j]); } } } __syncthreads(); } // Apply alpha #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < WM / MMA_M; ++i) { #pragma unroll for (int j = 0; j < WN / MMA_N; ++j) { #pragma unroll for (int t = 0; t < cFrag[i][j].num_elements; ++t) { cFrag[i][j].x[t] *= alpha; } } } // Store to D int warpRow = warpId / WARPS_N; The outputs only depend on the logical values of B, not its physical layout. So if we store B in column-major but with the same logical values as the reference's row-major B, the outputs will match. The question is: do the logical values match? For a standard PyTorch reference, B logical values at position (k, n) are some random integers. Our kernel reads B at `k + n*K` (column-major offset). If the reference's physical tensor is row-major with offset `k*N + n`, and we create our B with column-major layout but the same logical values (i.e., B[k, n] is the same for both), then our `B[k + n*K]` in the kernel correctly reads B[k,n]. So the key is: we must create a B tensor such that when accessed as column-major `B[k + n*K]`, it contains the same logical values as the reference's B at (k,n). The easiest way to achieve this: create B as standard (row-major), then transpose it if needed for our kernel, or create it directly as column-major with the right values. Actually, if our kernel expects column-major access `B[k + n*K]`, we can simply store B as a standard PyTorch tensor of shape (N, K) and access it transposed? No, our kernel takes `B` as a tensor and accesses it with linear index `k + n*K`. The physical memory layout must match this. So we need B's memory layout to be column-major. We can achieve this by: 1. Creating a standard tensor `B_temp = torch.randint(-4, 5, (K, N), dtype=torch.int8)` on CPU. 2. Then making it column-major by transposing and making contiguous: `B_temp.t().contiguous().t()`. Actually: ```python B_temp = torch.randint(-4, 5, (N, K), dtype=torch.int8) # shape (N, K) row-major B = B_temp.t() # shape (K, N), strides (1, K) -> column-major ``` But `B_temp` is on CPU. Then `B.cuda()` will move it. Wait, if we just do: ```python B_data = torch.randint(-4, 5, (K * N,), dtype=torch.int8) B = torch.as_strided(B_data, (K, N), (1, K)) ``` This creates a column-major view. The values are exactly the same as `B_data` which was generated by `torch.randint` on CPU. For matching the reference, if the reference does: ```python self.B = torch.randint(-4, 5, (K, N), dtype=torch.int8).cuda() ``` Then `self.B` is row-major. The element at (k, n) is `B_data[k * N + n]`. Our column-major B has element at (k, n) as `B_data[k + n * K]`. The values are DIFFERENT unless K == N or the data happens to match. Since K = N = 4096, they are equal! Wait, K = 4096 and N = 4096. If K == N, then row-major offset `k * N + n` equals column-major offset `k + n * K` only if `k = n`? No, `k * N + n` vs `k + n * K`. Since K = N = 4096, `k * 4096 + n` vs `k + n * 4096`. These are different unless k=0 or n=0. So the values are different. But we can create our column-major B by using the same base data but with column-major strides. Actually, `torch.randint` creates a flat array. For standard shape (K, N), it's interpreted as row-major. If we reinterpret the same flat array as column-major, the logical values at (k, n) change. To match the reference's logical values at (k, n), we need to either: 1. Know the reference's layout and match it. 2. Or create B such that `B[k, n]` in our tensor equals `B[k, n]` in the reference. If reference B is row-major standard, then `B[k, n]` is at flat index `k * N + n`. To create a column-major tensor where `B[k, n]` equals that same value, we need to create a tensor with shape (K, N) and column-major strides (1, K), with the flat data arranged accordingly. If the reference `B_ref` was created by `torch.randint(-4, 5, (K, N))`, then `B_ref` is a flat array `data_ref` of length K*N. `B_ref[k, n] = data_ref[k * N + n]`. We want our `B` to be column-major (strides (1, K)) with `B[k, n] = data_ref[k * N + n]`. Column-major tensor `B` with shape (K, N) and strides (1, K) stores its data as `B[k, n] = flat_data[k + n * K]`. So we need `flat_data[k + n * K] = data_ref[k * N + n]`. This means `flat_data` is the transpose of `data_ref` (when interpreted as KxN row-major). So: `B = torch.randint(-4, 5, (K, N), dtype=torch.int8).t().contiguous()` gives shape (N, K). Then to get back to (K, N) column-major: `B = B.t()`. Wait: ```python B_row = torch.randint(-4, 5, (K, N), dtype=torch.int8) # shape (K,N), row-major, values as desired B_col = B_row.t().contiguous().t() # shape (K,N), column-major, logical values = B_row.T ``` That's wrong. `B_col` would have logical values equal to `B_row.T`, not `B_row`. To get column-major tensor with the same logical values as a row-major tensor: ```python B_row = torch.randint(-4, 5, (K, N), dtype=torch.int8) # logical values we want B_col = B_row.t().contiguous() # shape (N, K), row-major, storing the transpose # Wait, that's a (N,K) tensor. Our kernel expects (K,N). ``` If our kernel expects B to be shape (K, N) with column-major strides, we can do: ```python B_col = B_row.t().contiguous().t() # shape (K, N), but now logical values are transposed! B_col[k, n] = B_row[n, k] ``` That's not what we want. Actually, let's think differently. Our kernel computes `D[i,j] = sum_k A[i,k] * B[k,j]`. It accesses B as `B[k + j * K]`. For this to compute the correct matrix product, the physical memory at offset `k + j*K` must contain the logical value `B[k,j]`. If we create B as a standard row-major tensor with shape (K, N), then `B[k,j]` is stored at offset `k * N + j`. Our kernel reads offset `k + j * K`. So we'd be reading the wrong data. To fix this without changing the kernel, we need B's physical layout to be column-major. That is, the tensor must have strides (1, K). How to create such a tensor with the same logical values as a standard `torch.randint(-4, 5, (K, N))`? Option: create the standard tensor, then permute the data. ```python B_std = torch.randint(-4, 5, (K, N), dtype=torch.int8) B_col_data = B_std.t().contiguous() # shape (N, K), row-major, data is transpose of B_std B_col = B_col_data.as_strided((K, N), (1, K)) # reinterpret as (K, N) column-major ``` Let's check: `B_col` has shape (K, N) and strides (1, K). Its underlying data is `B_col_data` which is `B_std` transposed (flattened). `B_col[k, n]` is at flat index `k + n * K` in `B_col_data`. `B_col_data` is the transpose of `B_std`, so element at (n, k) in `B_std` is at flat index `n * K + k` in `B_std`, which equals `k + n * K` in `B_col_data` (since K*N = N*K). Wait, `B_std` is row-major (K, N), so element (n, k) is at `n * N + k`. That's not `k + n * K`. Actually, `B_std.t().contiguous()` creates a tensor of shape (N, K) that is row-major. Element (n, k) in this tensor corresponds to `B_std[k, n]`. The flat index of (n, k) is `n * K + k`. If we reinterpret this as shape (K, N) with strides (1, K), then element (k, n) has flat index `k + n * K`. But we want `B_col[k, n] = B_std[k, n]`. `B_std[k, n]` = element at row k, col n of B_std. `B_std.t().contiguous()[n, k] = B_std[k, n]`. The flat index of `B_std.t().contiguous()[n, k]` is `n * K + k`. So if `B_col[k, n]` accesses flat index `k + n * K`, and this equals `n * K + k` only if K=N and... no, `k + n * K` always equals `n * K + k`. They are the same expression! So yes, `flat_idx = k + n * K = n * K + k`. So `B_col[k, n]` accesses the same flat index as `B_std.t().contiguous()[n, k]`, which equals `B_std[k, n]`. Great! So: ```python B_std = torch.randint(-4, 5, (K, N), dtype=torch.int8) B_col = B_std.t().contiguous().as_strided((K, N), (1, K)) ``` will create a column-major (K, N) tensor with the same logical values as the standard row-major `B_std`. Actually, let's verify with a small example: K=2, N=3. B_std = [[a,b,c],[d,e,f]] (row-major). Flat: [a,b,c,d,e,f]. B_std.t() = [[a,d],[b,e],[c,f]] (shape 3x2). Flat: [a,d,b,e,c,f]. Now as_strided((2,3), (1,2)): B_col[0,0] -> flat[0+0*2]=flat[0]=a. Good, equals B_std[0,0]. B_col[0,1] -> flat[0+1*2]=flat[2]=b. Good, equals B_std[0,1]. B_col[0,2] -> flat[0+2*2]=flat[4]=c. Good. B_col[1,0] -> flat[1+0*2]=flat[1]=d. Good, equals B_std[1,0]. B_col[1,1] -> flat[1+1*2]=flat[3]=e. Good. B_col[1,2] -> flat[1+2*2]=flat[5]=f. Good. Perfect! This works for K=2, N=3. For general K, N: `B_std.t().contiguous()` has shape (N, K), flat layout: B_std[0,0], B_std[1,0], B_std[2,0], ..., B_std[K-1,0], B_std[0,1], ... Wait, no. `B_std.t()` is (N, K). Element (n, k) = B_std[k, n]. Flattened row-major: for n=0: B_std[0,0], B_std[1,0], ..., B_std[K-1,0]. For n=1: B_std[0,1], B_std[1,1], ... So flat index of B_std[k, n] is `n * K + k`. As_strided((K, N), (1, K)) accesses flat index `k * 1 + n * K = k + n * K`. This equals `n * K + k`. Perfect match! So this is the correct way to create a column-major B with the same logical values as a standard row-major B. Now, does the hidden reference use row-major or column-major? For correctness with respect to a standard PyTorch matmul, B must be standard row-major. But our kernel expects column-major. We can change our kernel to expect row-major B! For matrix multiplication `D = A * B` using tensor cores: - A is MxK, B is KxN. - WMMA `matrix_a` can be row_major. - WMMA `matrix_b` can be row_major or col_major. - If B is row-major in shared memory, we use `wmma::load_matrix_sync(bFrag, ptr, ldm, wmma::mem_row_major)`. - The leading dimension would be BN (the width of the tile). But wait, in the standard GEMM algorithm, B is loaded as a KxN tile. In shared memory, we can store it either row-major or column-major. The global loads should be coalesced. For B row-major in global memory: To coalesce loads, we want threads to load contiguous elements in a row (varying n). This is actually easier than column-major. Global load pattern for B (row-major, KxN): Each row has BN=256 bytes. A warp can load 512 bytes (32 threads * 16 bytes). So a warp can load 2 rows. For 8 warps, 16 rows per load round. BK=64, so 64/16 = 4 load rounds. This is also coalesced! And since B is row-major globally, this is more natural and doesn't require any transposition in the Python code. So maybe I should rewrite the kernel to treat B as row-major! This eliminates all the layout confusion and makes it compatible with standard PyTorch. Let me redesign the kernel for B row-major. Global B: shape (K, N), row-major. Element (k, n) at `B[k * N + n]`. Shared memory sB: shape (BK, BN), row-major. Element (k, n) at `sB[k * BN + n]`. WMMA load for matrix_b row_major: `wmma::load_matrix_sync(bFrag[j], reinterpret_cast<signed char*>(&sB[k * BN + n]), BN, wmma::mem_row_major);` Wait, is row_major supported for matrix_b with signed char? Looking at mma.hpp: `fragment<matrix_b, 16, 16, 16, signed char, row_major>` - yes, I see it in the overload list. And `load_matrix_sync(fragment<matrix_b, 16, 16, 16, signed char, row_major>& a, const signed char* p, unsigned ldm)` - yes, I see that too (3-parameter version without layout_t). Great! This is simpler and more compatible with standard PyTorch. So I'll use: - A: row-major (MxK) -> sA row-major (BM x BK) - B: row-major (KxN) -> sB row-major (BK x BN) - D: row-major (MxN) -> store as row-major WMMA config: - aFrag: matrix_a, row_major - bFrag: matrix_b, row_major - cFrag: accumulator, row_major store This computes the standard matrix product. Now let's verify the coalesced load pattern for B row-major. B tile is BK x BN = 64 x 256. Each row has 256 bytes = 16 int4s. Total rows = 64. With 8 warps, each warp handles 8 rows (64/8). Each warp has 32 threads. Each thread loads one int4 (16 bytes). 32 threads * 16 bytes = 512 bytes per warp = 2 rows. Wait, 32 threads loading 16 bytes each = 512 bytes. Each row is 256 bytes. So 512 bytes = 2 rows. But I said each warp handles 8 rows. With 2 rows per load, that's 4 loads per warp. Pattern: ``` for (int row_group = 0; row_group < 4; ++row_group) { int row = warpId * 8 + row_group * 2 + (lane >> 4); // lane / 16 gives 0 or 1 int col_int4 = lane & 15; // lane % 16 gives 0..15 int globalRow = bk + row; int globalCol = bn + col_int4 * 16; int src_offset = globalRow * N + globalCol; int dst_offset = row * BN + col_int4 * 16; reinterpret_cast<int4*>(sB + dst_offset) = reinterpret_cast<const int4*>(B + src_offset); } ``` Wait, `lane >> 4` gives 0..1. So for row_group=0: lane 0..15: row = warpId*8 + 0 + 0 = warpId*8, col_int4 = lane lane 16..31: row = warpId*8 + 0 + 1 = warpId*8 + 1, col_int4 = lane-16 So for a warp: - 16 threads load row `warpId*8`, columns 0..15*16 (first 256 bytes = entire row). - 16 threads load row `warpId*8+1`, columns 0..15*16. Within a warp, addresses are: lane 0: B[(bk+warpId*8)*N + bn + 0] lane 1: B[(bk+warpId*8)*N + bn + 16] ... lane 15: B[(bk+warpId*8)*N + bn + 240] lane 16: B[(bk+warpId*8+1)*N + bn + 0] ... lane 31: B[(bk+warpId*8+1)*N + bn + 240] These are: - Lanes 0..15: contiguous addresses in row 0, skipping by 16 bytes each. Actually, lane 0 loads bytes 0..15, lane 1 loads bytes 16..31. These are perfectly contiguous! So the first 16 threads access a contiguous 256-byte range (one full row). - Lanes 16..31: contiguous addresses in row 1. - Between lane 15 and lane 16: stride of N - 240 + 0 = N - 240. For N=4096, that's 3856 bytes. So the warp issues 2 transactions of 256 bytes each (or 4 of 128, etc). This is very efficient. And 4 row_groups cover warpId*8 to warpId*8+7. Perfect. For A row-major (BM x BK = 128 x 64): Each row has 64 bytes = 4 int4s. 128 rows total. 256 threads = 8 warps. Each warp handles 16 rows (128/8). Each warp load: 32 threads * 16 bytes = 512 bytes = 8 rows. So 2 loads per warp. Pattern: ``` for (int row_group = 0; row_group < 2; ++row_group) { int row = warpId * 16 + row_group * 8 + (lane >> 2); // lane / 4 gives 0..7 int int4Id = lane & 3; // lane % 4 gives 0..3 int globalRow = bm + row; int globalCol = bk + int4Id * 16; int src_offset = globalRow * K + globalCol; int dst_offset = row * BK + int4Id * 16; reinterpret_cast<int4*>(sA + dst_offset) = reinterpret_cast<const int4*>(A + src_offset); } ``` Wait, `lane >> 2` gives 0..7. So for row_group=0: lane 0..3: row = warpId*16 + 0 + 0 = warpId*16, int4Id = 0..3 lane 4..7: row = warpId*16 + 0 + 1 = warpId*16+1, int4Id = 0..3 ... lane 28..31: row = warpId*16 + 0 + 7 = warpId*16+7, int4Id = 0..3 So a warp loads 8 rows, 4 int4s per row. For row_group=1: lane 0..3: row = warpId*16 + 8 + 0 = warpId*16+8 ... lane 28..31: row = warpId*16 + 15, int4Id = 0..3 This is coalesced! For each row, 4 threads load 4 contiguous int4s = 64 bytes contiguous. But the threads handling the same row are interleaved with threads handling other rows. In a warp: lane 0: row 0, offset 0 lane 1: row 0, offset 16 lane 2: row 0, offset 32 lane 3: row 0, offset 48 lane 4: row 1, offset 0 ... lane 31: row 7, offset 48 Within the warp: - Lanes 0-3 access row 0, offsets 0, 16, 32, 48. Contiguous 64 bytes. Perfect coalescing. - Lanes 4-7 access row 1, offsets 0, 16, 32, 48. Contiguous 64 bytes in the next row. - Gap between lane 3 (ending at offset 63) and lane 4 (starting at row1+offset0 = K) is K-64 bytes = 4032 bytes. So 8 transactions of 64 bytes each per warp. Very good. OK, this is a solid kernel design with row-major B and A. Let me write the complete code for this. Actually wait, I need to double-check: does wmma with row-major B compute the correct thing? Let's verify. The MMA instruction computes: D = A * B + C. When we do `wmma::load_matrix_sync(aFrag, sA_ptr, BK, row_major)` and `wmma::load_matrix_sync(bFrag, sB_ptr, BN, row_major)`, and then `wmma::mma_sync(cFrag, aFrag, bFrag, cFrag)`, the math should be correct for the matrix product. Let me draft the Python/CUDA code carefully: ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline cpp_sources = """ #include <torch/extension.h> torch::Tensor gemm_forward(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, int32_t alpha, int32_t beta); """ cuda_sources = """ #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <mma.h> using namespace nvcuda; #define BM 128 #define BN 256 #define BK 64 #define WM 64 #define WN 64 #define MMA_M 16 #define MMA_N 16 #define MMA_K 16 #define WARPS_M 2 #define WARPS_N 4 __global__ void __launch_bounds__(256) gemm_int8_wmma( const int8_t* __restrict__ A, const int8_t* __restrict__ B, const int32_t* __restrict__ C, int32_t* __restrict__ D, int M, int N, int K, int32_t alpha, int32_t beta) { __shared__ __align__(16) int8_t sA[BM * BK]; __shared__ __align__(16) int8_t sB[BK * BN]; int bm = blockIdx.x * BM; int bn = blockIdx.y * BN; int tid = threadIdx.x; int warpId = tid >> 5; int lane = tid & 31; // A coalesced load // Each warp handles 16 rows (128/8). reasoning
The compilation timed out, likely because of the large kernel or compilation overhead. Let me simplify the kernel significantly. The reference is `D = alpha * A * B + beta * C` with A (M, K) row-major, B (K, N) column-major int8, output D (M, N) int32. Wait, ORIGINAL CUTLASS code has: - LayoutInputA = RowMajor - LayoutInputB = ColumnMajor - LayoutOutput = RowMajor So `B` in the original is stored column-major. But torch tensors are row-major by default. So how should I handle this? In the original CUTLASS: - A is (M, K) with row-major strides (K, 1) - B is (K, N) with column-major strides (1, K) So B's layout in memory is column-major: consecutive elements in a column are contiguous, and rows are separated by stride K. In torch, to create a column-major tensor of shape (K, N), we can use `.t()` on a row-major (N, K) tensor, or set strides explicitly. In my ModelNew init, `B` should be initialized with shape (K, N) but with column-major memory layout: `torch.empty_strided((K, N), (1, K), dtype=torch.int8)`. I need to be more careful about this. Also, I should simplify the kernel to avoid compilation timeout. Perhaps use a simpler GEMM kernel without WMMA to get things working first, or use a much simpler WMMA kernel with fewer templates. Actually, let me try a fully unrolled kernel without template definitions inside the nvcc source, and reduce compile times. Also, since the problem mentions "optimized for FP16", the int8 mode in the original is just what CUTLASS used. But on H100, maybe we should use FP16? Wait, the I/O contract says inputs are `torch.int8` and we need to match the hidden reference. The reference still expects int8. Let me write a much simpler kernel. I'll use direct shared memory tiling with integer accumulators, and avoid the WMMA template complexity that leads to long compile times. Actually, let me check: H100 HO supports 16x8x16 MMA for int8 with .mma.sync.aligned.m64n8k16.row.col.s32.s8.s8.s32. Wait, can't easily use PTX. OK let me just write a very simple shared memory GEMM kernel for int8 -> int32 that compiles quickly, and move towards correctness first. Critical points: - A: row-major (M, K), contiguous columns - B: column-major (K, N), contiguous rows (since column-major means B[k, n] and B[k+1, n] are adjacent in memory) - C, D: row-major (M, N) In a simple tiling kernel: - thread block computes BM x BN tile of D - Loads BK x tile from A and B into shared memory - Each thread computes several elements For simplicity, let me do: - BM=64, BN=64, BK=32 - block=256 threads - 4x4=16 thread tiles of 4x4 elements each? Or each thread computes 1 element. Actually, simplest correct kernel: each thread computes 1 element of D. - blockDim = (16, 16) for a 16x16 tile - gridDim = (M/16, N/16) - Each thread computes dot product of row of A and column of B But that would be extremely slow. We need something faster. Let me try a medium-performance kernel with shared memory: - BM=128, BN=128, BK=32 - block=256 threads - warp of 32 threads cooperates - use vectorized int4 loads Actually, let me drastically simplify to ensure compilation passes within the timeout, and then optimize. Here is a simple but correct kernel: ```cuda __global__ void gemm_simple(const int8_t* A, const int8_t* B, const int32_t* C, int32_t* D, int M, int N, int K, int32_t alpha, int32_t beta) { int row = blockIdx.y * 128 + threadIdx.y; int col = blockIdx.x * 128 + threadIdx.x; if (row < M && col < N) { int32_t sum = 0; for (int k = 0; k < K; ++k) { sum += int32_t(A[row * K + k]) * int32_t(B[k + col * K]); } D[row * N + col] = alpha * sum + beta * C[row * N + col]; } } ``` But this would be very slow since only 1 element per thread and no shared memory, plus small block size. However, compile time would be tiny. To make it somewhat reasonable but still compile quickly: - blockDim=(16,16), each thread computes 8x8 tile? No, that needs loops. Actually, compile timeout suggests the previous nvcc compilation of the wmma-heavy kernel took too long. Maybe because of WMMA header instantiations with many template parameters. Let me avoid WMMA and use a straightforward shared-memory kernel. That compiles much faster.
